[n6f166b7ee5b] sub6
  Which one of the following pairs is NOT correctly matched?
  Vaishali — Monkey's honey offering | Rajagriha — Taming of Nalagiri | Shravasti — Twin Miracle | *Sankasya — First Buddhist Council
  x: Sankasya is associated with the descent of Buddha from Trayastrimsa heaven. The First Buddhist Council was held at Rajagriha.
[nindian-history-50-9] sub6
  Which group consists of places associated with Buddhist council traditions?
  Rajasthan, Gujarat, Delhi and Kashmir | *Rajgir, Vaishali, Pataliputra and Kashmir | Sarnath, Bodh Gaya, Kushinagar and Bihar | Bihar, Sarnath, Rajgir and Gujarat
  x: The four Buddhist councils were held at Rajgir, Vaishali, Pataliputra and Kashmir. Sarnath, Bodh Gaya and Kushinagar are linked with the Buddha's life, not the councils.
[f99dd850bd1e2] sub6
  Where did the Twin Miracle of the Buddha take place?
  Rajagriha | *Shravasti | Vaishali | Sankasya
  x: The Twin Miracle took place at Shravasti, the Kosala capital.
[f9dca5b9fe7cb] sub6
  With which place is the Buddha's descent from the Trayastrimsa heaven associated?
  Shravasti | Vaishali | *Sankasya | Sarnath
  x: Sankasya is the site of the descent from the Trayastrimsa heaven.
[fa1451275e62f] sub6
  With which place is the taming of the elephant Nalagiri associated?
  Vaishali | Shravasti | Kushinagar | *Rajagriha
  x: The Buddha tamed the elephant Nalagiri at Rajagriha (Rajgir).
[n83e4937aa6d] sub6
  Which one of the following pairs of a royal disciple of the Buddha and his kingdom is NOT correctly matched?
  Bimbisara - Magadha | Prasenajit - Kosala | Udayana - Kausambi | *Ajatashatru - Kosala
  x: Bimbisara and Ajatashatru ruled Magadha, Prasenajit ruled Kosala and Udayana ruled Kausambi. So Ajatashatru - Kosala is wrong.
[ff98c964cff38] sub6
  Which Kosala ruler became a disciple of the Buddha with his family?
  Bimbisara | Udayana | *Prasenjit | Ajatashatru
  x: Prasenjit of Kosala became a disciple with his family; Bimbisara and Ajatashatru ruled Magadha and Udayana Vatsa.
[n874d2cee3f9] sub7
  Which one of the following pairs is correctly matched in relation to the Four Noble Truths?
  Dukkha — The path to cessation | *Samudaya — The cause of suffering | Nirodha — Craving and attachment | Magga — The permanence of worldly life
  x: Samudaya identifies the cause of suffering, especially craving, ignorance and attachment. Dukkha is suffering, Nirodha is its cessation and Magga is the path.
[f625365292c01] sub7
  What does 'Samudaya' refer to in the Four Noble Truths?
  Cessation of suffering | Path to end suffering | *Cause of suffering | Existence of suffering
  x: Samudaya is the cause (craving, ignorance, attachment); Nirodha is cessation.
[gcc3d18c66a8d] sub7
  Which of the Four Noble Truths refers to Nirvana, the cessation of suffering?
  Dukkha | Samudaya | *Nirodha | Magga
  x: Nirodha, the third Noble Truth, is the cessation of suffering, that is Nirvana; Samudaya is its cause and Magga the path.
[nf9d1fdb7fc9] sub7
  To which group of the Eightfold Path does Right Mindfulness belong?
  Ethical conduct | Wisdom | *Mental discipline | Monastic rules
  x: Right Mindfulness is part of mental discipline; ethics consists of Right Speech, Right Action and Right Livelihood.
[ne76ef93106e] sub7
  In the Eightfold Path, 'Sankappa' means Right:
  View | Speech | *Intention | Concentration
  x: Sankappa is Right Intention; Samma-ditthi is Right View, Vaca Right Speech and Samadhi Right Concentration.
[fd61c03b8291b] sub7
  Which group of the Eightfold Path is formed by Right Speech, Right Action and Right Livelihood?
  Prajna (wisdom) | Samadhi (mental discipline) | Dhamma (teaching) | *Sila (ethics)
  x: These three make up Sila; Right View and Right Intention form Prajna.
[f55098fad4979] sub7
  Which components of the Eightfold Path make up Prajna (wisdom)?
  *Right View and Right Intention | Right Effort and Right Concentration | Right Speech and Right Action | Right Mindfulness and Right Livelihood
  x: Prajna comprises Right View (samma-ditthi) and Right Intention (sankappa).
[f322ecfe7db16] sub8
  Which symbol represents the Great Renunciation in early Buddhist art?
  *Horse | Bodhi tree | Wheel | Stupa
  x: The horse symbolises the Great Renunciation; the Bodhi tree stands for enlightenment.
[fc28005da76f7] sub8
  Which of the following pairs of event in the Buddha's life and symbol is correctly matched?
  Enlightenment – Stupa | Mahaparinirvana – Horse | *Birth – Lotus and bull | First sermon – Bodhi tree
  x: Birth is shown by the lotus and bull; enlightenment by the Bodhi tree and Mahaparinirvana by the stupa.
[f884bbf6ef3a4] sub8
  Which symbol shows the Mahaparinirvana of the Buddha in early aniconic art?
  Wheel | Horse | Lotus | *Stupa
  x: The stupa stands for the Mahaparinirvana; the wheel for the first sermon.
[f0d5328a3fc3e] sub8
  Which symbol represents the Buddha's first sermon in early Buddhist art?
  Lotus and bull | *Wheel (Dharmachakra) | Bodhi tree | Stupa
  x: The wheel (Dharmachakra) marks the first sermon at Sarnath.
[n85492606448] sub9
  What is the Buddhist doctrine that every phenomenon arises owing to a cause called?
  Nirodha | *Pratityasamutpada | Anicca | Anatta
  x: Pratityasamutpada is the doctrine of dependent origination, according to which phenomena arise through causal conditions.
[n5a936a22a91] sub9
  Which is the first link in the chain of Pratityasamutpada?
  Samskara | Vijnana | *Avidya | Namarupa
  x: The first four links run Avidya, Samskara, Vijnana and Namarupa, so Avidya (ignorance) comes first.
[fee7e9b30bd02] sub9
  How many links (nidanas) make up the chain of Pratityasamutpada?
  8 | *12 | 10 | 14
  x: Pratityasamutpada is a 12-link chain of nidanas.
[f02d31d7e10c7] sub9
  Which is the last link in the chain of Pratityasamutpada?
  Jati | Avidya | *Jara-marana | Bhava
  x: The chain ends with Jara-marana (old age and death), after Bhava and Jati; Avidya is the first link.

=== NOTES 1:29 | Buddhist Councils, Hinayana–mahayana–vajrayana, Tripitakas and Pali Literature
## SUB 0 | The Four Buddhist Councils – Place, Patron, President
[29:0:0] TABLE head: Council | Place / year | Patron | President | Outcome
  [29:0:0.0] First | Rajagriha (Rajgir), Saptaparni Cave; c. 483 BCE, soon after the Buddha's death | Ajatashatru (Haryanka) | Mahakassapa | Vinaya (recited by Upali) and Sutta (by Ananda) compiled
  [29:0:0.1] Second | Vaishali; 383 BCE, 100 years after the Buddha's death | Kalashoka (Shishunaga) | Sabakami (Sabbakami) | Dispute on monastic rules; split into Sthaviravadins and Mahasanghikas
  [29:0:0.2] Third | Pataliputra (Ashokarama monastery); c. 250 BCE | Ashoka (Maurya) | Moggaliputta Tissa | Abhidhamma Pitaka, Kathavatthu; heretics expelled; missions abroad
  [29:0:0.3] Fourth | Kundalavana, Kashmir; 1st c. CE | Kanishka (Kushana) | Vasumitra (Ashvaghosha deputy) | In Sanskrit; Mahavibhasa commentaries; division into Mahayana and Hinayana
[29:0:1] - **Order of venues:** **Rajagriha → Vaishali → Pataliputra → Kundalavana** = codes "2, 1, 4, 3" (UPPCS 2025; UP Lower 2002).
[29:0:2] - **Presidents (match):** 1st **Mahakassapa** – 2nd **Sabakami** – 3rd **Moggaliputta Tissa** – 4th **Vasumitra** → "1-(b), 2-(a), 3-(d), 4-(c)" (APPSC DAO 2020, FRO 2020).
[29:0:3] - **Patrons in order:** **Ajatashatru → Kalashoka → Ashoka → Kanishka**.
[29:0:4] - **First council:** presided by **Mahakassapa** (not Ananda, Upali, Ajatashatru or Nagasena); Ajatashatru was the patron.
[29:0:5] - **Third council:** at **Pataliputra**, in the **Ashokarama** monastery; recorded only in the Sri Lankan **Dipavamsa and Mahavamsa** – not in Ashoka's inscriptions or the Divyavadana (UPPCS 1999).
[29:0:6] - **Fourth council:** held on the advice of the monk **Parsva**; settled **Sarvastivada** disputes of Kashmir–Gandhara; after it Buddhism split into **Mahayana and Hinayana** (SSC 2018; IAS 1993).
[29:0:7] > Chronology of rulers: Ashoka (3rd c. BCE) → Milinda/Menander (2nd c. BCE) → Kanishka (1st–2nd c. CE).
[29:0:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.67-68, p.72; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.50, p.61; Rocket Indian History Part 2 p.25, p.115; Mahavamsa ch. 5
## SUB 1 | Buddhist Scholars – Nagarjuna, Ashvaghosha, Buddhaghosa, Bhavaviveka
[29:1:0] TABLE head: Scholar | School / fact | Work
  [29:1:0.0] Nagarjuna | Founder of Madhyamika (Shunyavada) of Mahayana; doctrine of Shunyata (void); friend of a Satavahana king | Mulamadhyamakakarika, Suhrllekha, Ratnavali (Rajaparikatha)
  [29:1:0.1] Ashvaghosha | Kanishka's court poet; deputy at the 4th council | Buddhacharita (Sanskrit), Saundarananda
  [29:1:0.2] Buddhaghosa | Theravada, 5th c.; born near Guntur (Kota Nemapuri); worked at Anuradhapura | Visuddhimagga (Pali); Samantapasadika
  [29:1:0.3] Bhavaviveka | Madhyamika; acharya at the Dhanyakataka (Amaravati) vihara | Tarkajvala
  [29:1:0.4] Aryadeva | Nagarjuna's disciple; Madhyamika – a Buddhist, not Jaina scholar | Chatuhshataka
  [29:1:0.5] Dignaga (Dinnaga) | Buddhist logician; "Father of Indian logic"; Yogachara | Pramana Samuccaya
  [29:1:0.6] Dharmakirti (7th c.) | "Kant of India"; developed Dignaga's logic; taught at Nalanda | Pramana Vartika
  [29:1:0.7] Asanga, Vasubandhu | Brothers (4th c.); Yogachara | Vasubandhu – Abhidharmakosa
  [29:1:0.8] Nagasena | Dialogue with Menander | Milindapanho
  [29:1:0.9] Sanghabhuti | Monk in China, 381–384 CE | Commentary on the Sarvastivada Vinaya
[29:1:1] - **Nagarjuna – welfare state:** set out the theory of the welfare state (**Shreyorajya**) in the **Ratnavali** (Rajaparikatha-ratnavali), addressed to a Satavahana king (APPSC AP History).
[29:1:2] - **Nagarjuna** formulated the **basic ideas of Mahayana** (NDA 2014) and taught **Shunyata** (UPPCS 1998; CDS 2006) – not Nagasena, Ashvaghosha or Shankaracharya.
[29:1:3] - **Suhrllekha** – letter to a friend; **I-tsing** saw students memorising it. Title "**Einstein of India**" – idea of relativity.
[29:1:4] - **Sriparvata:** royal friend **Yajna Sri Satakarni** built the **1,500-room Maha Vihara (Parvata Vihara)** for Nagarjuna.
[29:1:5] - **Siddha Nagarjuna** (Vajrayana alchemist at Sriparvata, later period) is a **different person** from the philosopher.
[29:1:6] - **Bhavaviveka = Dhanyakataka** (NextGen; Hiuen Tsang) – a printed key of "Sriparvatam" (APPSC G2 2019) is wrong.
[29:1:7] - **Wrong pair:** "**Nagarjuna – Buddhacharita**" (Ashvaghosha wrote it); Hala – Gathasaptasati, Vatsyayana – Kamasutra, Gunadhya – Brihatkatha are right.
[29:1:8] - **UPSC 2022:** Aryadeva – Jaina ✗, Dignaga – Buddhist ✓, **Nathamuni – Vaishnava** ✓ → **only two pairs** correct.
[29:1:9] > Visuddhimagga = Buddhaghosa (not Nagarjuna, Vasubandhu, Padmasambhava, Dignaga).
[29:1:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 2 p.8, p.21-22; NextGen AP History Part 2 p.13, p.18-19; NextGen AP History Part 6 p.14; NextGen previous papers p.13, p.27; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.57, p.61, p.66-67, p.81; old app notes 1:29
## SUB 2 | Hinayana, Mahayana, Vajrayana and the Bodhisattvas
[29:2:0] TABLE head: Sect | Ideal / features | Buddha | Texts / spread
  [29:2:0.0] Hinayana / Theravada | Arhat – individual liberation; original teachings | Symbols, no idols; a great man, not a god | Pali canon; Sri Lanka, Myanmar, Thailand; Ashoka patronised it
  [29:2:0.1] Mahayana (after the 4th council, Kanishka) | Bodhisattva – delays own nirvana to help all beings; Sunyata | Image worship – Buddha as god | Sanskrit – Prajnaparamita, Lotus Sutra; China, Japan, Korea, Tibet; Kanishka, Harsha patrons
  [29:2:0.2] Vajrayana ("vehicle of the thunderbolt") | Salvation through magical power (vajra); mantras, mudras, mandalas | Taras, wrathful deities | Guhyasamaja, Arya Manjushrimulakalpa; Bengal–Bihar, Tibet, Nepal
[29:2:1] - **Fundamental difference:** Mahayana made **images of the Buddha and worshipped him as God**; Hinayana did not (UPPCS 1996). The Buddha was **elevated to God in Kanishka's period**.
[29:2:2] - **Vajrayana:** emerged in the **7th century CE** (APPSC AP History), flourished from the 8th c.; **most prominent in the Sultanate period** (JPSC 2013); centre **Vikramashila**; linked with **Padmasambhava**. In Andhra: **Salihundam**.
[29:2:3] - **Arya Manjushrimulakalpa** = **Vajrayana** (Mahayana tantra; MPPSC 2023 key also accepted Mahayana).
[29:2:4] - **Bodhisattva** – central to **Mahayana, not Hinayana**; a compassionate one on the way to enlightenment who **delays his own salvation** to help all beings (UPSC 2016: B and C only).
[29:2:5] - **Six paramitas** (perfections) of the Bodhisattva: **dana, sila, kshanti, virya, dhyana, prajna**.
[29:2:6] - **Avalokiteshvara** = **Padmapani** ("lotus-bearer") = Lokeshvara, Bodhisattva of **compassion** (IAS 1997). **Maitreya** = the **future Buddha** yet to come (UPSC 2018). Manjushri = wisdom; Vajrapani = power.
[29:2:7] - **Sthaviravadins** = Hinayana (not Mahayana); **Lokottaravadins** = offshoot of the **Mahasanghikas**; Mahasanghika **deification of the Buddha** fostered Mahayana (UPSC 2020: II and III).
[29:2:8] - **Satavahana-period Andhra:** **Madhyamika, Mahayana and Hinayana** (Chaityaka sects) – not Zen or Theravada (APPSC ATWO).
[29:2:9] - **Tibetan** Buddhism merges Mahayana and Vajrayana with Bon; Mahayana reached Tibet in the 7th c.; **Gelug** school dominant by the 15th c.; canon = **Kangyur and Tengyur**. **Karmapa Lama** = **Kagyu (Kangyupa)** sect, seat Rumtek (Sikkim) (UPPCS 2000).
[29:2:10] > Arhat = Hinayana; Bodhisattva = Mahayana; vajra, Taras, Guhyasamaja = Vajrayana.
[29:2:11] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.68-70; NextGen Part 2 p.21; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.54-59; PMF Art & Culture Part 5 p.79; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.122; old app notes 1:29
## SUB 3 | Tripitaka – Vinaya, Sutta, Abhidhamma and the Nikayas
[29:3:0] TABLE head: Pitaka | Content | Books / compiler
  [29:3:0.0] Vinaya | Rules and discipline of the Sangha (monastic life) | Patimokkha (227 rules for monks), Vibhanga, Khandhaka; Upali
  [29:3:0.1] Sutta | Buddha's sermons on ethics and doctrine (Buddhavacana) | 5 Nikayas; Ananda
  [29:3:0.2] Abhidhamma | Philosophical analysis of the Dhamma | 7 books; added at the 3rd council (Moggaliputta Tissa)
[29:3:1] - **Tripitaka** = the collection of the Buddha's teachings, the **Buddhist** canon (not Jain, Sikh or Hindu); written in **Pali**.
[29:3:2] - **Patimokkha** = the **rules of the Sangha** (CDS 2009), recited every fortnight.
[29:3:3] - **Seven Abhidhamma books:** **Dhammasangani** (first), Vibhanga, Dhatukatha, Puggalapannatti, **Kathavatthu**, **Yamaka**, **Patthana** (seventh). Yamaka belongs to the **Abhidhamma** (JPSC 2016).
[29:3:4] TABLE head: Nikaya (Sutta Pitaka) | Content
  [29:3:4.0] Digha | Long discourses in Pali; Mahaparinibbana Sutta; Samannaphala Sutta
  [29:3:4.1] Majjhima | Middle-length discourses
  [29:3:4.2] Samyutta | Grouped discourses
  [29:3:4.3] Anguttara | Arranged by number; lists the 16 Mahajanapadas
  [29:3:4.4] Khuddaka | 15 books: 550 Jatakas, Dhammapada, Suttanipata, Buddhavamsa, Theragatha, Therigatha
[29:3:5] - **Branches of Buddhist literature:** **Pitakas and Jatakas**; the **Angas** are the **Jain** canon (CDS 2006).
[29:3:6] - **Early literature** is generally **canonical**, but schools do not class their canon as "only the Pitakas" (NDA 2014: only I).
[29:3:7] > 16 Mahajanapadas = Anguttara; Jatakas, Dhammapada = Khuddaka; Kathavatthu, Yamaka = Abhidhamma.
[29:3:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 2 p.6-8; NextGen Part 3 p.31; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.47-54; PMF Art & Culture Part 5 p.5; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.131
## SUB 4 | Non-canonical Texts – Language and Sect
[29:4:0] TABLE head: Text | Language | Note
  [29:4:0.0] Milindapanho (Milinda-Panha) | Pali | Dialogue of monk Nagasena and Indo-Greek king Milinda (Menander)
  [29:4:0.1] Dipavamsa, Mahavamsa | Pali | Sri Lankan chronicles; Ashoka, councils; Mahavamsa by Mahanama
  [29:4:0.2] Nettipakarana, Visuddhimagga | Pali | Visuddhimagga by Buddhaghosa
  [29:4:0.3] Divyavadana | Sanskrit | Buddhist legends (Ashoka)
  [29:4:0.4] Avadanashataka | Sanskrit | Avadana tales
  [29:4:0.5] Arya Manjushrimulakalpa | Sanskrit | Mahayana–Vajrayana, historical data
  [29:4:0.6] Buddhacharita | Sanskrit | Ashvaghosha – the Buddha's biography
  [29:4:0.7] Lalitavistara | Buddhist hybrid Sanskrit | Buddha biography; source of Edwin Arnold's The Light of Asia
  [29:4:0.8] Mahavastu | Mixed Sanskrit–Prakrit | Biography of the Buddha (Lokottaravada)
  [29:4:0.9] Dohakosa | Apabhramsa | Songs of the Vajrayana siddhas – the last Buddhist text produced in India (SSC 2011)
  [29:4:0.10] Vinayavatthu | Pali | Mentions the "Andhrapatha"
[29:4:1] - **Language of propagation:** **Pali**, the people's language (SSC 2017); Sanskrit came with Mahayana.
[29:4:2] - **Sanskrit group:** Divyavadana and Manjushrimulakalpa (RAS 2024: 1 and 4); **Pali group:** Dipavamsa, Mahavamsa, Milindapanho.
[29:4:3] - **Account of Ashoka:** the **Mahavamsa** (CDS 2018), not the Pitakas.
[29:4:4] > Divyavadana is Sanskrit, Milindapanho is Pali; Mahavamsa is by Mahanama, not Ashvaghosha.
[29:4:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 2 p.5-8; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.60-63; Britannica 'Saraha', 'The Light of Asia'; old app notes 1:29
## SUB 5 | Philosophical Schools – Sarvastivada to Yogachara
[29:5:0] TABLE head: School | Founder / teacher | Doctrine
  [29:5:0.0] Sthaviravada | Elders at the 2nd council | Orthodox, strict Vinaya → Theravada
  [29:5:0.1] Mahasanghika | Mahadeva's group | Relaxed rules; parent of Chaitika, Lokottaravada and Mahayana
  [29:5:0.2] Vaibhashika (Sarvastivada) | Based on the Mahavibhasa | Dharmas exist in past, present and future ("all exists")
  [29:5:0.3] Sautrantika | Kumaralata | Relies on the Suttas; Hinayana
  [29:5:0.4] Madhyamika / Shunyavada | Nagarjuna; Aryadeva, Buddhapalita, Bhavaviveka | Sunyata (emptiness); midway between realism and idealism
  [29:5:0.5] Yogachara / Vijnanavada | Maitreyanatha, Asanga, Vasubandhu | Only consciousness is real
[29:5:1] - **Nagarjuna's school = Madhyamika** (UK UDA 2007) – not Sautrantika, Vaibhashika or Yogachara.
[29:5:2] - **Aryadeva** = Madhyamika (not Yogachara); Yogachara and Vijnanavada are one school.
[29:5:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.69-70; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.54, p.57-59; old app notes 1:29

=== NOTES 1:30 | Spread and Decline of Buddhism; Buddhist and Jain Architecture – Stupa, Chaitya, Vihara
## SUB 0 | Buddhist Learning Centres and Monasteries
[30:0:0] TABLE head: Centre | Founder / dynasty | Location | Note
  [30:0:0.0] Nalanda | Kumaragupta I (Gupta), c. 427 CE | Bihar | Mahayana university; Dharmakirti taught; sacked by Bakhtiyar Khalji; UNESCO site
  [30:0:0.1] Vikramashila | Dharmapala (Pala), 770–810 | Antichak, Bhagalpur, Bihar | Main centre after Nalanda's decline; later Vajrayana centre
  [30:0:0.2] Somapura Mahavihara | Dharmapala (Pala) | Paharpur (Bangladesh) | UNESCO site
  [30:0:0.3] Odantapuri | Gopala (Pala) | Bihar | –
  [30:0:0.4] Vallabhi | Maitrakas | Gujarat | Maitraka capital; Hinayana centre
  [30:0:0.5] Nagarjunakonda (Sriparvata-Vijayapuri) | Ikshvakus | Andhra | Famous educational centre under the Ikshvakus
  [30:0:0.6] Dhanyakataka (Amaravati) | Satavahanas | Andhra | Bhavaviveka
[30:0:1] - **Dharmapala** was called "**Param Saugat**"; **Haribhadra** was in his court; the **Palas** were great patrons of Buddhism (IAS 2006).
[30:0:2] - **Not a Buddhist learning centre:** **Nagpur** (Vallabhi, Vikramashila, Nalanda were); Varanasi, Girnar, Ujjain are distractors.
[30:0:3] - **Ikshvakus** were **not antagonistic** to Buddhism – their queens patronised Nagarjunakonda.
[30:0:4] - Vikramashila's later fame for Vajrayana is **not why** Dharmapala founded it.
[30:0:5] TABLE head: Himalayan monastery / institute | State
  [30:0:5.0] Tawang – largest in India, 2nd largest in the world ("Asia's oldest and biggest" in the RRB key) | Arunachal Pradesh
  [30:0:5.1] Tabo (996 CE, "Ajanta of the Himalayas") | Himachal Pradesh (Spiti)
  [30:0:5.2] Pemayangtse (Nyingma), Rumtek (Karmapa) | Sikkim
  [30:0:5.3] Central Institute of Buddhist Studies | Leh (Choglamsar, Ladakh)
[30:0:6] - **Bodh Gaya's 80-ft Great Buddha** statue (1989) was built by the **Japanese** (Daijokyo sect).
[30:0:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.68; PMF Art & Culture Part 5 p.25-29, p.78-80; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 3 p.24; NextGen Ikshvakus p.3, p.5; cibs.ac.in; old app notes 1:29-30
## SUB 1 | Buddhist Sites and Sect Centres of Andhra Pradesh
[30:1:0] TABLE head: Site | District | Key features
  [30:1:0.0] Amaravati (Dhanyakataka) | Guntur / Palnadu, on the Krishna | Largest stupa in AP; site most linked to Satavahana patronage; identified by Colin Mackenzie (1797); Amaravati School – white limestone; Fergusson: "Epitome of Indian Architecture"
  [30:1:0.1] Bhattiprolu | Guntur (now Bapatla) | Oldest stupa in AP; Yaksha kings Savaru and Kuberaka; inscribed (crystal) relic caskets
  [30:1:0.2] Guntupalli | West Godavari (Eluru) | Only rock-cut cave temple and oldest chaitya in AP; Rajagirika centre
  [30:1:0.3] Jaggayyapeta | NTR (old Krishna), on the Krishna | Early relic stupa; Uttarasaila centre
  [30:1:0.4] Nagarjunakonda (Sriparvata / Vijayapuri) | Palnadu | Ikshvaku capital; "Dakshina Gaya"; island in Nagarjuna Sagar; Aparasaila centre
  [30:1:0.5] Salihundam | Srikakulam, on the Vamsadhara | Mahayana–Vajrayana; Tara bronzes; "Tibet of AP"; found by Gidugu Ramamurthy (1919)
  [30:1:0.6] Thotlakonda, Bavikonda | Visakhapatnam | Hilltop monasteries; the word "Tambaya Danam" is noticed at a Visakhapatnam Buddhist site
  [30:1:0.7] Sankaram (Bojjannakonda) | Anakapalli | Rock-cut caves; found by Alexander Rea, 1906
  [30:1:0.8] Ghantasala (Kantakasala) | Krishna | Ancient port
  [30:1:0.9] Bhairavakonda | Nellore | Eight caves
  [30:1:0.10] Adurru / Chandavaram | Konaseema / Prakasam | Adurru – only Divya stupa; Chandavaram – Mahastupa + 115 small stupas
[30:1:1] - **Vijayapuri** – Buddhist centre of the **3rd–4th c. CE**, now **submerged under Nagarjuna Sagar** (APPSC ZSWO).
[30:1:2] - **Sect centres:** **Purvasaila – Dhanyakataka (Amaravati)**; **Aparasaila – Sriparvata (Nagarjunakonda)**; **Rajagirika – Guntupalli**; **Siddharthaka Chaitya – Gudivada** (APPSC G2 2008).
[30:1:3] - **Wrong pair (APPSC G2 2011): "Guntupalli – Siddharthaka Chaitya"** (Guntupalli = Rajagirika). ⚠ Some scholars place the Siddharthikas at Guntupalli.
[30:1:4] - **Mahasanghika** sect was born at **Dhanyakataka**; **9 of the 18** early sects existed in Andhra; **Andhakas** attended the 3rd council (Kathavatthu); Hinayana – Amaravati, Mahayana – Sriparvata, Vajrayana – Salihundam.
[30:1:5] - **N. Venkataramanayya** identified "**Sriparvata Swamy**" with the Buddha (APPSC G2).
[30:1:6] - **Why monasteries near cities** (e.g. Amaravati near Guntur): monks lived on **daily alms**, so viharas stood close enough to towns for the alms round (merchant donations also flowed).
[30:1:7] - **Latest:** AP plans **2 Buddhist circuits** round its **40** monuments (2024–25); Feb 2026 "**Discover Andhra**" campaign – Amaravati, Bavikonda, Thotlakonda.
[30:1:8] - **Distractors:** Kondaveedu – **Reddy** kingdom; Srinatha's **Haravilasam** – Reddi sea trade.
[30:1:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP History Part 2 p.11, p.21; NextGen AP History Part 6 p.11, p.14; NextGen AP History Part 7 p.15; NextGen previous papers p.10, p.13; Rocket AP History p.8; NextGen Ikshvakus p.3; old app notes 1:30
## SUB 2 | Major Stupas – Largest, Oldest, Location
[30:2:0] TABLE head: Stupa | Location | Builder / period / fact
  [30:2:0.0] Piprahwa | UP (ancient Kapilavastu) | Among the earliest, c. 5th c. BCE; inscribed relic vessel
  [30:2:0.1] Sanchi | Raisen, MP | Ashoka's brick stupa (3rd c. BCE); stone casing under the Shungas; four toranas by the Satavahanas; UNESCO 1989; rediscovered 1818 (General Taylor); not linked to any event of the Buddha's life
  [30:2:0.2] Bharhut | Satna, MP | Shunga, 2nd c. BCE; stone railing; found by Cunningham, 1873
  [30:2:0.3] Amaravati | AP | Satavahana Mahachaitya (Pulumavi II's time); largest stupa in AP and South India
  [30:2:0.4] Devnimori | near Shamlaji, Aravalli district, Gujarat | Western Kshatrapas, c. 4th c. CE; relic casket; relics taken to Sri Lanka in Feb 2026
  [30:2:0.5] Dhamek | Sarnath, UP | Gupta, c. 500 CE; marks the first sermon
  [30:2:0.6] Kesariya | East Champaran, Bihar | ASI: largest and tallest excavated stupa (c. 104 ft) – key "largest stupa in the world" (UP RO 2023)
  [30:2:0.7] Borobudur | Java, Indonesia | Largest Buddhist monument in the world; UNESCO
[30:2:1] - **Largest stupa:** **Borobudur** (world, NextGen); **Kesariya** when Borobudur is not offered; NextGen gives **Sanchi** as largest in India; **Amaravati** largest in South India (APPSC).
[30:2:2] - **Event sites:** Bodh Gaya – enlightenment; Sarnath – first sermon; Kusinara – death; **Sanchi – none** (UPPCS 2011).
[30:2:3] - **"Most ancient stupa" (own-MCQ angle):** **Sanchi** (Ashokan, 3rd c. BCE, one of the oldest stone structures in India) is older than Bharhut (2nd c. BCE).
[30:2:4] - **Relics** of the Buddha were divided among **nine stupas** after his death.
[30:2:5] - **Sarnath:** B. R. Mani's excavations (2013–14) showed activity **before Ashoka**; on the UNESCO **Tentative List since 1998**, nominated in 2025–26.
[30:2:6] - **Recent digs:** **Vaishya Tekri** (near Ujjain) – believed a Mauryan stupa linked to **Ashoka's wife Devi**, possibly larger than Sanchi; **Ratnagiri (Jajpur, Odisha)** – ASI resumed in Dec 2024, colossal Buddha head found; Odisha's **Diamond Triangle = Ratnagiri, Udayagiri, Lalitagiri**.
[30:2:7] > Chronology: Piprahwa → Sanchi → Devnimori → Dhamek.
[30:2:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP History Part 2 p.11; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.68, p.137; PMF Art & Culture Part 5 p.81; ASI Kesariya stupa; UNESCO 'Buddhist Monuments at Sanchi'; old app notes 1:29-30
[30:2:9] - **Kanaganahalli** (Bhima river, **Kalaburagi**, Karnataka): Buddhist stupa site excavated by the **ASI, 1994–2001**; 1st–3rd c. CE stupa with the only inscribed stone portrait of **Ashoka** ("Raya Asoko"); now under ASI conservation.
## SUB 3 | Stupa, Chaitya and Vihara – Concept and Parts
[30:3:0] - **Stupa** = solid relic mound worshipped by circumambulation; the **concept is pre-Buddhist** (burial mounds; the word occurs in Vedic texts).
[30:3:1] - **Buddhist stupas** enshrine **relics**, are built as **objects of devotion** by monasteries and **commemorate events** of the Buddha's life – all four statements true (APPSC AEE flashcard). UPSC 2023: "Buddhist in origin" ✗, relic repository ✓, votive and commemorative ✓ → **only two**.
[30:3:2] - **Chaitya** (chaitya-shala / chaitya-griha) = **place of worship**, prayer hall with a stupa at the apsidal end – the Buddhist "temple" (**chaitya stupas**).
[30:3:3] - **Vihara** = **residence of monks**; **Arama** = complex of **stupa + vihara + chaitya + school**, e.g. Nandaluru.
[30:3:4] TABLE head: Part | Meaning
  [30:3:4.0] Anda | Hemispherical dome (the cosmos)
  [30:3:4.1] Medhi | Raised drum / terrace round the base of the dome, for circumambulation
  [30:3:4.2] Harmika | Square railing on top of the anda
  [30:3:4.3] Yashti | Central mast rising from the harmika
  [30:3:4.4] Chhatra / Chhatri | Umbrella(s) on the yashti
  [30:3:4.5] Vedika | Stone railing round the stupa
  [30:3:4.6] Torana | Ornamental gateways at the four directions
  [30:3:4.7] Pradakshinapatha | Path for clockwise circumambulation
  [30:3:4.8] Ayaka pillars / Ayakapata | Special feature of Andhra stupas
[30:3:5] - **Anda, harmika and chhatri** = parts of a **stupa** (APPSC G2 2024).
[30:3:6] TABLE head: Stupa type | Content
  [30:3:6.0] Dhatugarbha | Bodily relics (Amaravati, Bhattiprolu)
  [30:3:6.1] Paribhogika | Objects used by the Buddha
  [30:3:6.2] Uddeshika | Commemorative, no relics
  [30:3:6.3] Aniconic symbol | Event
  [30:3:6.4] Lotus and bull | Birth
  [30:3:6.5] Horse (Kanthaka) | Renunciation
  [30:3:6.6] Empty seat under the Bodhi tree | Enlightenment
  [30:3:6.7] Dharmachakra (wheel) | First sermon
  [30:3:6.8] Stupa / footprints | Parinirvana
[30:3:7] - **Hinayana** showed the Buddha by **symbols**; **Mahayana** adopted images; first human images – **1st c. CE, Gandhara and Mathura**.
[30:3:8] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.135-137; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 2 p.27, p.33; PMF Art & Culture Part 3 p.40, p.63-65; old app notes 1:30
## SUB 4 | Rock-cut Caves – Buddhist and Jain
[30:4:0] TABLE head: Cave | State | Religion | Feature
  [30:4:0.0] Barabar (Lomas Rishi) | Bihar | Ajivika (Ashoka) | Mauryan, 3rd c. BCE; Ashokan inscriptions
  [30:4:0.1] Bhaja | Maharashtra | Buddhist cave shrine | Early
  [30:4:0.2] Karla | Maharashtra | Buddhist | Largest Hinayana chaitya (Satavahana)
  [30:4:0.3] Nasik (Pandavleni) | Maharashtra | Buddhist | Satavahana; Gautami Balashri inscription
  [30:4:0.4] Kanheri | Salsette, Mumbai | Buddhist | Over 100 caves (109); 11-headed Avalokiteshvara
  [30:4:0.5] Ajanta | Maharashtra | Buddhist | 25 viharas + 4 chaityas; Cave 1 Padmapani; Cave 10 Svetagaja Jataka (not 11); Cave 17 Vakataka cycle; paintings mainly Gupta / post-Gupta
  [30:4:0.6] Bagh | MP | Buddhist | Gupta; Cave 4 "Rang Mahal"
  [30:4:0.7] Ellora | Maharashtra | Hindu, Buddhist, Jain | 34 caves: 17 + 12 + 5; Kailasa – Rashtrakutas
  [30:4:0.8] Elephanta | off Mumbai | Shaiva | Trimurti Shiva
  [30:4:0.9] Udayagiri-Khandagiri | Odisha | Jain | Kharavela
  [30:4:0.10] Sittanavasal | Tamil Nadu | Jain cave shrine | Paintings
[30:4:1] - **Besnagar** (Vidisha) is known for the **Bhagavata Heliodorus (Garuda) pillar**, not a Shaivite cave shrine → UPSC 2023: **only two pairs** correct.
[30:4:2] - **Western India rock-cut caves:** **Nasik, Ellora and Ajanta** (IAS 1993 key).
[30:4:3] - **Cave chronology:** Barabar → Karla → Bagh → Kailasa (Ellora).
[30:4:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.67, p.83; PMF Art & Culture Part 3 p.43-44; NextGen AP History Part 2 p.11; old app notes 1:30
## SUB 5 | Spread, Patrons and Decline of Buddhism
[30:5:0] - **Causes of spread:** simple doctrine, appeal to the lower orders, **missionary** zeal and the people's **language** (Pali/Prakrit) – I, II, III, IV (UPPSC 2009); not "soundness of the Vedic spirit".
[30:5:1] - **Mauryan followers:** **Ashoka** and **Dasharatha** (CGPSC 2013); Chandragupta became a Jain, Bindusara an Ajivika.
[30:5:2] - **Sites (IAS 1998):** **Lothal – dockyard**, **Sarnath – first sermon**, **Nalanda – learning** ✓; Rajgir – Lion Capital ✗ (the capital is from Sarnath).
[30:5:3] TABLE head: Ashoka's missionary | Region
  [30:5:3.0] Mahinda + Sanghamitta | Sri Lanka
  [30:5:3.1] Sona + Uttara | Suvarnabhumi
  [30:5:3.2] Majjhantika | Kashmir and Gandhara
  [30:5:3.3] Mahadeva | Mahishamandala (Mysore)
  [30:5:3.4] Rakkhita | Vanavasi
  [30:5:3.5] Maharakkhita | Yona country
[30:5:4] - Buddhism reached Central Asia and China by the Kushan-era **Silk Route**.
[30:5:5] - **Mahavamsa:** **Mahadeva Bhikshu** took Buddhism **from Andhra to Sri Lanka** (not the reverse).
[30:5:6] TABLE head: Andhra patron | Contribution
  [30:5:6.0] Satavahanas | Vedic kings but lavish donors; Sanchi toranas
  [30:5:6.1] Veerapurushadatta (Ikshvaku) | Nagarjunakonda Maha Chaitya; royal women donors
  [30:5:6.2] Upasika Bodhisri | Service in Ehuvala Chantamula's reign
  [30:5:6.3] Vishnukundins | Last dynasty to support Buddhism in Andhra; Indrapalanagara vihara
[30:5:7] - Greatest royal patronage in Andhra: **Ikshvakus** of Vijayapuri. **Lay patronage** – queens, merchants, guilds; 2,000 karshapanas with a **weavers'** guild for robes.
[30:5:8] - **Decline:** loss of royal patronage; **Brahmanical (Bhakti) revival**; Vajrayana ritualism; **Sanskrit replaced Pali**; hostile rulers **Pushyamitra Shunga, Shashanka, Mihirakula**; Turkish raids (Nalanda sacked by **Bakhtiyar Khalji**). **Hiuen Tsang** found Sriparvata **without patronage**.
[30:5:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.66; NextGen Part 2 p.43; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.123; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.68; old app notes 1:29-30

=== NOTES 1:31 | Other Heterodox Sects – Ajivikas (makkhali Gosala), Charvaka / Lokayata Materialism
## SUB 0 | Ajivikas – Makkhali Gosala and Niyati
[31:0:0] - **Founder (exam answer):** **Makkhali Gosala** (Gosala Maskariputra) – its most important leader and populariser (UPPCS 1996; UKPSC 2024; NDA 2015); earlier teachers like Nanda Vaccha are also named. Not Ananda, Upali, Vasubandhu or Dignaga.
[31:0:1] - **Niyati (fate):** "**Fate assigns everything, man is powerless**" = **Ajivikas** (UPPCS 2006; RAS 2013) – not Jains, Buddhists or Mimamsakas.
[31:0:2] - All thoughts and actions are **fixed before birth**; **no free will**; karma cannot change destiny; the **order of rebirth cannot be changed**.
[31:0:3] - **Practice:** belief in **determinism** and **severe austerities** (RAS 2024: 1 and 3); ascetics called **Sudra sannyasins**; **no caste discrimination**; held meetings in regular **sabhas** (State PSC: A, B and D).
[31:0:4] - **Atomic theory** of matter – influenced the later **Vaisheshika**.
[31:0:5] - Gosala was **Mahavira's companion for about six years**; Jain texts call him Mahavira's **disciple**; the **Bhagavati Sutra** records their quarrel; the **Majjhima Nikaya** records the Buddha's debates with Jains and Ajivikas.
[31:0:6] TABLE head: Ruler / record | Ajivika link
  [31:0:6.0] Bindusara | Popular under him; joined the sect
  [31:0:6.1] Ashoka, Dasharatha | Gifted the Barabar (and Nagarjuni) caves – among the earliest rock-cut caves in India
  [31:0:6.2] 7th Pillar Edict (Delhi-Topra) | Names Ajivikas with Brahmanas, the Sangha and Nirgranthas
[31:0:7] - Barabar caves were given by **Ashoka, not Chandragupta**; Ashoka himself was Buddhist.
[31:0:8] - **Decline:** survived about **2,000 years**, to the **14th c. CE** in **Karnataka and Tamil Nadu**; faded with the Puranic-Bhakti revival and loss of patronage.
[31:0:9] > Niyativada = Gosala (Ajivika); materialism = Charvaka.
[31:0:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.38, p.50; NextGen Part 2 p.43, p.45; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.84-85; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.123; old app notes 1:31
## SUB 1 | Charvaka / Lokayata Materialism
[31:1:0] - **Other names:** **Lokayata** / **Lokayata Darshan** ("of the common people") (State PSC; CGPSC 2018); **Barhaspatya** after the legendary founder **Brihaspati**.
[31:1:1] - **Complete materialism**; highest value = **Kama** (pleasure) – not Dharma, Artha or Moksha (CGPSC 2017).
[31:1:2] - **Dehatmavada:** "**The conscious body is the soul**" (CGPSC 2024).
[31:1:3] - **Only pramana = perception** (pratyaksha) (CGPSC 2018); rejects inference, Vedic testimony, ritualism and the afterlife. Nyaya accepts **four** pramanas.
[31:1:4] - **Aphorism (IAS 1994):** "**Yavaj jivet sukham jivet, rinam kritva ghritam pibet**" – live well while you live, even on borrowing; once cremated there is no return → **Charvakas**.
[31:1:5] - Original **Brihaspati Sutra** is lost; Lokayata is known through **rivals' criticism**, e.g. **Madhava's Sarvadarshanasangraha**.
[31:1:6] TABLE head: School | Karma | Soul
  [31:1:6.0] Jainism | Removable by penance | Eternal jiva in all things
  [31:1:6.1] Buddhism | Intention-based | Anatta
  [31:1:6.2] Ajivika | Cannot alter destiny | Accepted
  [31:1:6.3] Charvaka | Does not decide rebirth | Conscious body
[31:1:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.50; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.123; Rocket Part 2 p.5; PMF Art & Culture Part 5 p.105; old app notes 1:31
## SUB 2 | Six Teachers and the Age of Religious Ferment
[31:2:0] - **6th century BCE:** an age of religious upheaval across the world, while the **Vedic religion had become complex** – both true (UKPSC 2012). Shramana movements arose amid **urbanisation and Magadhan patronage**.
[31:2:1] TABLE head: Teacher | Doctrine
  [31:2:1.0] Makkhali Gosala | Niyativada – fatalism (Ajivika)
  [31:2:1.1] Purana Kassapa | Akriyavada – actions bring neither merit nor demerit
  [31:2:1.2] Ajita Kesakambalin | Materialist – nothing survives death
  [31:2:1.3] Pakudha Kaccayana | Seven eternal, uncreated elements
  [31:2:1.4] Sanjaya Belatthiputta | Sceptic – "eel-wriggling"
  [31:2:1.5] Nigantha Nataputta | = Mahavira
[31:2:2] - Source: the **Samannaphala Sutta** of the **Digha Nikaya** – Ajatashatru reports the six teachers' views to the Buddha.
[31:2:3] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.86-87; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.50; old app notes 1:31
[31:2:4] - **Why the heterodox sects arose:** the Vedic religion in **Sanskrit** was beyond common people; costly **yajnas** and animal sacrifice consumed the cattle needed for iron-plough farming; **Kshatriyas** resented Brahmin dominance (Mahavira and the Buddha were Kshatriyas); newly rich **Vaishyas** sought higher status and backed the new sects, not Vedic sacrifice.

  x: The Ajivikas held an atomic theory of matter that influenced the later Vaisheshika. The seven eternal elements belong to Pakudha Kaccayana, dependent origination (Pratityasamutpada) is Buddhist, and the Ajivikas did not hold that matter is unreal.

  x: Sanskrit-centred religious practice, along with costly yajnas, animal sacrifice and dissatisfaction with ritualism, encouraged heterodox sects.
[n635a09d9bb4] sub2
  Which of the following is NOT correct about the causes of the rise of heterodox sects?
  Kshatriyas resented Brahmin dominance | Vedic sacrifices consumed cattle needed for iron-plough farming | Mahavira and Buddha were both Kshatriyas | *Newly rich Vaishyas favoured the Vedic sacrificial religion
  x: The newly rich Vaishyas wanted higher status and did not favour the Vedic sacrificial religion; the other three statements are correct.
[fb0d615904e7d] sub2
  Consider the following statements about the rise of heterodox sects:
1. Kshatriyas resented the dominance of Brahmins.
2. Newly rich Vaishyas sought higher status.
3. Vedic sacrifices conserved the cattle needed for farming.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  *1 and 2 only | 2 and 3 only | 1 and 3 only | 1, 2 and 3
  x: Sacrifices consumed the cattle needed for iron-plough farming, so statement 3 is wrong.
[n101b4ee716a] sub2
  What did Purana Kassapa's doctrine of Akriyavada hold?
  All actions are fixed by fate before birth | The body is composed of four elements and nothing survives | Reality consists of seven eternal elements | *Actions produce neither merit nor demerit
  x: Akriyavada, associated with Purana Kassapa, held that actions have no moral consequence; even killing or charity produces neither merit nor demerit.
[n73cb71fa0d1] sub2
  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched?
  *Ajita Kesakambalin – Materialist | Sanjaya Belatthiputta – Ajivika | Makkhali Gosala – Materialist | Ajita Kesakambalin – Skeptic
  x: Ajita Kesakambalin represented materialism. Gosala led the Ajivikas and Sanjaya Belatthiputta was the skeptic.
[fc1a8dd023efb] sub2
  Which heterodox teacher held that there are seven eternal, uncreated elements?
  Ajita Kesakambalin | *Pakudha Kaccayana | Purana Kassapa | Sanjaya Belatthiputta
  x: Pakudha Kaccayana taught seven eternal, uncreated elements.
[f51c99b6df926] sub2
  Which teacher was a sceptic, known for 'eel-wriggling'?
  Makkhali Gosala | Ajita Kesakambalin | Pakudha Kaccayana | *Sanjaya Belatthiputta
  x: Sanjaya Belatthiputta would neither affirm nor deny, hence 'eel-wriggling'.
[g06729edfc0d3] sub2
  By what name is Mahavira referred to in Buddhist texts listing the heterodox teachers?
  *Nigantha Nataputta | Ajita Kesakambalin | Purana Kassapa | Sanjaya Belatthiputta
  x: Nigantha Nataputta of the Buddhist texts is Mahavira, the Jain teacher.
[n599dcc3cbfa] sub2
  In which text does Ajatashatru report the views of the six teachers to the Buddha?
  Brihaspati Sutra | *Samannaphala Sutta of the Digha Nikaya | Majjhima Nikaya | Bhagavati Sutra
  x: The Samannaphala Sutta, contained in the Digha Nikaya, presents Ajatashatru’s report of the views of the six teachers to the Buddha.
[n45e598c875c] sub2
  In which context did the Shramana movements of the sixth century BCE arise?
  Mauryan imperial patronage | The revival of Vedic ritualism in the Deccan | The Greek invasions of the north-west | *Urbanisation and Magadhan patronage
  x: The Shramana reaction (Jainism, Buddhism, Ajivika and Charvaka currents) arose amid urbanisation and Magadhan patronage in the sixth century BCE.

=== NOTES 1:32 | Persian and Macedonian Invasions – Alexander's Campaign, Battle of Hydaspes, Effects on India
## SUB 0 | Alexander's Campaign – Route, Years, Hydaspes, Retreat
[32:0:0] TABLE head: Event | Year
  [32:0:0.0] Crossed the Hindu Kush (start of the invasion) | 327 BC (APPSC G2 2008 key)
  [32:0:0.1] Entered through the Khyber Pass, crossed the Indus | 326 BC
  [32:0:0.2] Battle of the Hydaspes vs Porus | 326 BC
  [32:0:0.3] Halt at the Beas (Hyphasis) | 326 BC
  [32:0:0.4] Left India from Patala | 325 BC (about 19 months in India)
  [32:0:0.5] Died at Babylon | 323 BC, aged 32
[32:0:1] - **"When did Alexander invade?"** – **327 BC** when offered (APPSC); **326 BC** in UPPSC 2006 (Indus crossing, Hydaspes).
[32:0:2] - **Hydaspes** = Greek name of the **Jhelum** (Sanskrit **Vitasta**); Alexander defeated **Porus** there (UPRO 2013; APPSC G1 2002). Hyphasis = Beas, Acesines = Chenab, Hydraotes = Ravi.
[32:0:3] - **Porus** was **not executed** – asked how to be treated, he said "**Like a king**"; Alexander **restored and enlarged** his kingdom and made him ally/satrap.
[32:0:4] - **Bucephala** (after his horse) and **Nicaea** were founded on the Jhelum.
[32:0:5] - **Pushkalavati:** its Ashtaka raja **Astes (Hasti)** resisted and was killed – no alliance.
[32:0:6] - **Satrapies:** PMF – the land from the Indus to the Beas went into **three parts under Greek governors**; Telugu Akademi texts count **six satrapies** (hence the "a, b & d" key).
[32:0:7] - **Retreat:** army refused to cross the **Beas** (not the Ravi) – **war-weariness, homesickness, disease, fear of the Nanda army**; **12 stone altars** on its northern bank; **Nearchus** led the fleet down the Indus.
[32:0:8] - **A–R (UPPCS 1998):** Alexander left in 325 BC ✓; "defeated by Chandragupta Maurya" ✗.
[32:0:9] > Hydaspes = Jhelum = Vitasta; Hyphasis = Beas (halt point).
[32:0:10] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.94-96; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.78; Rocket Part 2 p.12, p.26, p.113; old app notes 1:32
## SUB 1 | India on the Eve of Alexander – Nandas and North-west Rulers
[32:1:0] - **Magadha:** **Nanda** dynasty ruled North India (IAS 2000); king **Dhana Nanda** (Greek **Agrammes**), the **last Nanda** and Alexander's contemporary – not Mahapadma Nanda or Chandragupta; commander **Bhaddasala**; huge army.
[32:1:1] - **North-west:** small independent states (Taxila, Porus's kingdom, Gandhara) – no united front.
[32:1:2] TABLE head: Ruler / people | Response
  [32:1:2.0] Ambhi (Omphis), Taxila | First to surrender; helped Alexander
  [32:1:2.1] Porus (Puru) – between Jhelum and Chenab | Resisted at the Hydaspes
  [32:1:2.2] Assakenoi (Ashvakas), capital Massaga | Women took up arms; queen Cleophis
  [32:1:2.3] Malloi (Malavas), Kshudrakas | Fought him during the retreat
[32:1:3] - **Takshashila (Taxila):** between the **Indus and the Jhelum**, near **Rawalpindi**, Pakistan (UPPCS 1996).
[32:1:4] TABLE head: Battle | Year | Between
  [32:1:4.0] Ten Kings (Dasharajnya) | – | Sudas vs ten kings, near the Parushni (Ravi); Rigveda
  [32:1:4.1] Kalinga War | 261 BC | Ashoka vs Kalinga
[32:1:5] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.37, p.95; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 2 p.40; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.78; old app notes 1:32
## SUB 2 | Achaemenid (Persian) Invasion and Its Influence
[32:2:0] - **Cyrus II** (c. 558–530 BC): first Achaemenid to campaign into the north-west – captured **Gandhara and Kamboja**.
[32:2:1] - **Darius I** (**522–486 BC**): usual answer for the **first Persian ruler to capture/annex Indian land** (RAS 1994) – Sindh and Punjab west of the Indus = **20th satrapy** (Hindush).
[32:2:2] - **Herodotus:** the most fertile satrapy; tribute of **360 talents of gold dust** (about one-third of the revenue).
[32:2:3] - **Influence:** **Kharosthi** from Aramaic, written **right to left**; Ashoka's **Kandahar** edict partly in **Aramaic**; words **lipi / dipi**; third-person preamble (Darius model); polished pillars; **satrapy** system.
[32:2:4] > First to campaign = Cyrus; first to annex (20th satrapy) = Darius I.
[32:2:5] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.94; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 2 p.53; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.76; Rocket Part 2 p.113; old app notes 1:32
## SUB 3 | Greek Writers and Effects of the Invasion
[32:3:0] TABLE head: Writer | Status
  [32:3:0.0] Nearchus (admiral), Onesicritus, Aristobulus | Came with Alexander; first-hand accounts
  [32:3:0.1] Arrian, Curtius, Plutarch | Later writers
  [32:3:0.2] Deimachus | Later envoy of Antiochus I to Bindusara – did not come with Alexander (UPPSC 2015)
[32:3:1] - **Effects:** direct contact and **new land and sea routes** between Greece and India; small NW states weakened, clearing the way for **Chandragupta Maurya**, who expelled the Greek satraps with **Porus (Parvataka)** as ally.
[32:3:2] - **Sandrokottas** = Chandragupta; the invasion is the **first dated event** of Indian history.
[32:3:3] - **Little lasting political mark:** the earlier Persian rule left the deeper influence.
[32:3:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.96-98, p.116; old app notes 1:32
